Not explicitly labeled 10b5-1 in the pool — **Inference, not from filings** | CIQ export, multiple Form 4s |

**Net insider activity: net buying, but the discretionary (real cash / real market) component is modest.** Stripping out option exercises, grant vesting, gifts, and sell-to-cover mechanics, the only genuine discretionary open-market transactions in the trailing 12 months are: Musk's $1.0bn buy (Sep-2025) against Kimbal Musk's $25.6M sale (Dec-2025) and Zhu's $7.3M sale (Sep-2025) — a net ~$967M of real conviction-driven buying, entirely from the CEO. The far larger dollar figures in the table (the $2.2bn Interim Award exercise, the $141.6bn Performance Award vesting, the $7.1bn cashless exercise) are compensation and award mechanics, not market conviction, and should not be read as "Musk bought $150bn of stock."

## 3. Pledging & Encumbrance

| Insider | Shares Pledged | % of Their Holding | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Elon Musk (CEO) | 207,498,721 sh "eligible to be pledged" as collateral for personal indebtedness (this is the disclosed ceiling, not necessarily the amount actually pledged) | 28.9% of his 717,112,739-share total beneficial stake (Dec-31-2025 basis) | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 12, footnote 2 |
| Kimbal Musk (Director) | 1,376,373 sh pledged as collateral for personal indebtedness | 100% of his disclosed stake | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 12, footnote 10 |

**Utilization is low despite the large notional.** As of December 31, 2025, based on directors' and officers' own written representations, the aggregate loan or investment amount actually collateralized by all pledged shares across the whole board and management team was **less than 1% of the total value of the pledged shares** [FY2025 10-K/A, Item 12, "Ownership of Securities" narrative]. A board policy caps CEO pledging-collateralized borrowing at the lesser of $3.5 billion or 25% of the pledged stock's value, and caps other directors/officers at 15% [FY2025 10-K/A, Item 11, "Pledging of Shares"].

**Residual denominator ambiguity (conservative default, CLAUDE.md §4).** The disqualifier-scan computed the "eligible to be pledged" ratio two ways: against Musk's full disclosed beneficial stake (717.1M shares) it is 28.9% — well below the 50% hard-disqualifier threshold; against only his directly-held trust shares (413,152,109, excluding option-derived shares the policy bars from pledging) it reads 50.2% — just over that threshold. The correct denominator under the standard formula is the person's total disclosed beneficial ownership, which reads 28.9%, so this does **not** meet the bar to trigger RF-OWN-001. The narrower reading is recorded here rather than silently discarded, and utilization (<1% of pledged value actually borrowed against) further limits the practical risk either way. This finding traces to the 10-K/A (a pool filing), so it is eligible to carry a red-flag designation, but it does not clear the materiality bar for one.

## 4. Control Structure

| Feature | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Dual-class / super-voting | None — Tesla has a single class of common stock; no super-voting share class identified anywhere in the pool | FY2025 10-K/A; management-governance/00_governance-data-triage.md §5A |
| Controlled-company status | Not formally designated a Nasdaq "controlled company" (requires >50% of voting power held by one person/group); Musk's 20.3–29.9% stake is well short of that formal bar, but functions as a founder-controlled company in practice given the board's deference in designing successive CEO awards | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 12; business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md |
| Shareholder bloc with board rights | None beyond the two Musk board seats (Elon Musk, CEO/Director; Kimbal Musk, Director) — no other bloc holder (BlackRock, Vanguard, State Street, Geode) has disclosed board-nomination or veto rights | FY2025 10-K/A, Director Independence section |
| Voting-rights mechanism unique to the CEO award | The 423,743,904 shares granted under the 2025 CEO Performance Award are subject to a Voting Agreement: while "Unearned," an irrevocable proxy is held by Tesla's corporate secretary and those shares vote proportionately to all other shareholders' votes (i.e. Musk does not control their vote today); voting control transfers to Musk as tranches are certified "Earned." As more of the 12 performance tranches vest (tied to market-cap milestones from $2.0 trillion upward), Musk's own discretionary voting power grows further, on top of an already-large economic stake | FY2025 10-K/A, CEO Performance Award description, p.3832–3852 |
| Controlling-owner objective alignment *(Filter 6)* | **Not triggered.** Tesla is not government-controlled, not a listed subsidiary of a value-maximizing parent, and not a sprawling unrelated-diversified conglomerate at the TSLA reporting-entity level — none of the three RF-OWN-004 structural categories applies. A related but distinct risk exists: Musk simultaneously runs SpaceX, xAI, X, The Boring Company and Neuralink, all of which compete for his time and several of which transact with Tesla ($405M revenue / $307M cost from SpaceX Megapack sales in H1-2026; a $2.0bn equity investment in SpaceX/xAI approved March 2026) [Q2 2026 10-Q, Note 13]. This is a divided-attention / related-party conflict, not a structural misalignment of the RF-OWN-004 type, and it is adjudicated in depth by `05_board-and-shareholder-rights` (independence, related-party governance) rather than capped here | FY2025 10-K/A; Q2 2026 10-Q, Note 13; business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md |

**Unaligned-owner test result:** RF-OWN-004 does not apply. No shareholder-friendliness structural cap is triggered by this test; the incentive-alignment and related-party questions raised by Musk's outside ventures are carried forward to the board/related-party module rather than being double-counted here.

## 4A. Market Conduct

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Insider buys/sells vs subsequent results (timing) | Tom Zhu's Sep-11-2025 open-market sale (20,000 sh, $7.3M, ~29.6% of his then-position) preceded the Q3 2025 print (reported ~late Oct-2025) by roughly six weeks; that quarter's EPS missed consensus by -10.71% [earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md, §6]. This is a real timing coincidence worth flagging, but the evidentiary strength is weak: one officer, a modest dollar amount, no proof of access to material non-public information, and no exchange or regulatory inquiry disclosed. Kimbal Musk's Dec-9-2025 open-market sale preceded the Q4 2025 print, which **beat** on both revenue (+0.49%) and EPS (+11.11%) — the opposite of a "sold ahead of bad news" pattern, so no flag there | CIQ Insider Trading export; earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md §6 |
| Unusual price / volume before announcement | Not proven from available data — this pool contains no trading-volume or price-anomaly analytics tied to specific announcement dates | — |
| Exchange clarification sought for a price move | Not proven from available data — no Nasdaq/SEC clarification request appears anywhere in the pool | — |
| Institutional (13F-equivalent) holding trend; any sharp high-quality-holder exit | Institutions hold 41.71% (1,647,507,082 sh); quarterly activity (Dec-2025→Mar-2026) shows 2,132 holders increasing vs 1,518 decreasing positions — roughly balanced, not a flight. One apparent large "exit" — The Vanguard Group, Inc. showing 0 shares, down (258,925,024) — is explained by a disclosed internal realignment: Vanguard's subsidiaries now report beneficial ownership separately rather than through the single Vanguard Group entity [FY2025 10-K/A, Item 12, footnote 3; CIQ Ownership Activity, "Top Buyers and Sellers"]. Reading this as a real institutional exit would be a misread of the reorganization; the underlying Vanguard-family position (Vanguard Capital Management, Vanguard Portfolio Management, Vanguard Fiduciary Trust, Vanguard Global Advisers — all appear as "new" buyers in the same window for this reason) is essentially unchanged | CIQ Public Ownership Summary, Ownership Activity + Top Buyers and Sellers tables; FY2025 10-K/A footnote 3 |

**Market-conduct flag.** The Zhu sale-before-a-miss pattern is recorded as a weak, Amber-level market-conduct observation (RF-MKT-001 candidate) rather than a confirmed red flag — it clears the pool-filing requirement (sourced from a Form 4 in the CIQ export) but does not clear the materiality bar for a Red verdict on the evidence available.

## 5. Ownership / Insider Read

Elon Musk owns a genuinely large stake — 20.3% as of the 10-K/A's Dec-2025 table, rising to 29.9% after his June-2026 option exercise — but that stake is overwhelmingly the product of three successive CEO performance-award grants (2012, 2018, 2025) rather than open-market buying; the one clear cash conviction signal in the last 12 months is a real ~$1.0 billion open-market purchase in September 2025, set against much smaller open-market sales by Kimbal Musk ($25.6M) and SVP Tom Zhu ($7.3M). Pledging is disclosed and bounded — 28.9% of Musk's stake is "eligible to be pledged" but under 1% of pledged value was actually borrowed against as of Dec-31-2025 — so it is a monitoring item, not a red flag. The biggest control risk to minority holders is not pledging or a dual-class structure (Tesla has neither); it is the compounding effect of the 2025 CEO Performance Award's Voting Agreement, which hands Musk full voting control over an additional 423.7 million shares as performance tranches are certified "Earned," on top of an already-concentrated economic and voting position and a network of transactions with the CEO's other controlled ventures.

## management-governance / 05_board-and-shareholder-rights.md

_Source: `05_board-and-shareholder-rights.md`_

# Board & Shareholder Rights — TSLA

Upstream input used: `01_management-and-track-record.md` (management stability, succession, CEO ownership context). Cross-module inputs used: `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md` (no hard disqualifier; related-party revenue below the 25% threshold), `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md` (net share count trajectory, related-party network, CEO award scale). Upstream output missing: `04_ownership-and-insider-behavior` — not yet produced at the time this agent ran; proceeding with this agent's own read of ownership data in the pool where it bears on voting rights.

## 1. Board Composition

| Feature | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Board size | 9 directors: Elon Musk, Robyn Denholm (Chair), Ira Ehrenpreis, Joe Gebbia, Jack Hartung, James Murdoch, Kimbal Musk, JB Straubel, Kathleen Wilson-Thompson | FY2025 10-K/A (filed Apr-30-2026), Item 10, director table |
| Independent directors (%) | 7 of 9 = 77.8%. The company discloses that every director is independent under Nasdaq standards **except** Elon Musk (CEO) and Kimbal Musk (Elon's brother) | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 10, "Director Independence" |
| Chair / CEO split? | Yes. Robyn Denholm has been independent Board Chair since November 2018, separate from CEO Elon Musk | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 10, Denholm biography |
| Audit Committee independence | 4 of 4 members independent: Robyn Denholm (Chair, designated "audit committee financial expert"), Joe Gebbia, Jack Hartung, James Murdoch | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 10, "Audit Committee" |
| Compensation Committee independence | 3 of 3 members independent: Ira Ehrenpreis (Chair), Robyn Denholm, Kathleen Wilson-Thompson | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 11, Compensation Committee section |
| Nominating & Corporate Governance / Disclosure Controls Committee independence | The filing states all four standing committees (Audit, Compensation, Nominating & Corporate Governance, Disclosure Controls) are "comprised solely of independent directors," but individual member-level assignment for these two committees is not cleanly extractable from the filing's summary table (a table-formatting limitation of the source document, not a disclosure gap the company created) | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 10, committee-membership table and narrative |

Board composition looks conventional on the independence headline (77.8%), and the two non-independent seats are transparently disclosed rather than hidden — but concentrating both non-independent seats in one family (CEO and his brother) on a 9-person board is a meaningful share of the table for a related pair, and the practical force of the seven "independent" directors is tested below (§2–§4).

## 2. Tenure & Refreshment

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Average director tenure | 11.1 years (calculated: Musk and Kimbal Musk since Apr-2004 [~22.1 yrs each], Ehrenpreis since May-2007 [~19.2 yrs], Denholm since Aug-2014 [~11.9 yrs], Murdoch since Jul-2017 [~9.0 yrs], Wilson-Thompson since Dec-2018 [~7.6 yrs], Gebbia since Sep-2022 [~3.8 yrs], Straubel since May-2023 [~3.2 yrs], Hartung since Jun-2025 [~1.1 yrs]; sum 100.0 yrs ÷ 9 directors) | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 10, individual director "has been a member of the Board since..." statements; calculation as of 2026-07-24 |
| Overboarding / staleness signs | CEO Elon Musk simultaneously serves as CEO/Chairman of SpaceX, CEO of xAI (a SpaceX subsidiary as of Feb-2026), CTO of X Corp, and founder/CEO roles at The Boring Company and Neuralink — a bandwidth concentration already flagged in `01_management-and-track-record.md`. Separately, director JB Straubel is the founder and CEO of Redwood Materials, a company that transacts commercially with Tesla (see §3) — he sits on the board overseeing a related-party counterparty he runs full-time. Director Kathleen Wilson-Thompson sits on three other large-company boards (Wolverine World Wide, McKesson, Health Care Service Corporation) in addition to Tesla. Two directors (Musk, Kimbal Musk) have 22-year tenures, well past the 10–12 year mark many governance frameworks flag for independence erosion | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 10, director biographies |
| Recent refreshment | Three of nine directors joined in the last four years: Joe Gebbia (Sep-2022), JB Straubel (May-2023), Jack Hartung (Jun-2025) — genuine turnover exists alongside the long-tenured core | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 10, director biographies |

**Read.** The board mixes real refreshment (3 of 9 seats turned over since 2022) with real staleness (a 22-year-tenured CEO and CEO's brother, a 19-year director) and a genuine independence-in-substance question for at least one nominally independent director (Straubel, whose own company is a Tesla counterparty). Musk's five-company outside workload is a stated, disclosed fact, not an inference — it is the single largest bandwidth question the board itself does not appear to have limited.

## 3. Related-Party Transactions & Group Leakage

| RPT Type | Amount | % of Revenue | % of PAT | % of Net Worth | Arm's Length? | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---|---|
| Sales to related parties (SpaceX vehicle sales + xAI Megapack sales) | $573.4M combined (SpaceX $143.3M + xAI $430.1M), FY2025; plus $78.2M (SpaceX $0.1M + xAI $78.1M) through Feb-2026 | ~0.6% of FY2025 revenue ($94,827M) | Not separately disclosed | — | Company states these are "at rates generally available to unaffiliated third parties under the same or similar circumstances" — asserted, not independently verified in this pool | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 13, "Related Person Transactions" |
| Purchases / expenses from related parties (SpaceX commercial/licensing, aircraft use, X advertising, xAI consulting, TBC, Redwood, CEO security firm) | ~$28.1M combined FY2025 (SpaceX $11.4M + $0.4M aircraft + X ads $3.3M + xAI $4.0M + TBC $0.9M + security firm $4.8M + Redwood $3.3M); ~$4.1M through Feb-2026 | <0.1% of FY2025 revenue | Not separately disclosed | — | Same RPT-policy assertion of arm's-length terms | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 13 |
| Equity investment in a related party (Tesla's $2.0bn purchase of SpaceX Class A common stock, via absorption of a prior xAI Holdings Series E commitment) | $2.0bn, completed Mar-12-2026 | n/a (investing, not revenue) | n/a | ~2.4% of net worth ($82,865M total equity, FY2025-end) | Company-approved under RPT Policy; no independent fairness opinion disclosed in this pool | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 13; `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md` |
| Personal-benefit RPT: CEO security services | $4.8M (2025) + $1.3M (through Feb-2026), paid to a security company owned by Elon Musk | <0.01% of revenue | — | — | Company-approved; a direct personal-benefit payment to a CEO-owned entity, distinct in character from an operating-supplier RPT | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 13 |
| Employment RPT | Jack Hartung's (a director) son-in-law employed at Tesla since Dec-2016, ~$150,000 FY2025 total compensation | Immaterial | — | — | Company states compensation set per standard practices for the role | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 13 |
| Related-party litigation | Three Delaware Court of Chancery derivative suits (filed May–Jun 2024) alleging breach of fiduciary duty tied to director dealings with X Corp. and xAI; dismissed by the trial court Apr-13-2026; plaintiffs appealed to the Delaware Supreme Court in May-2026 — unresolved | n/a | n/a | n/a | Unresolved — dismissal is on appeal, not a final resolution | Q2 FY26 10-Q, Note (Legal Proceedings), "Certain Derivative Lawsuits in Delaware" |

**Materiality read.** By the revenue test (MODULE_RULES thresholds), the recurring commercial RPTs are Low materiality (~0.6% of revenue, well under the 1% Low/5% Medium line). But the RPT picture is not really about size — it is about breadth and character: Tesla transacts with, and its CEO personally benefits from or leads, at least five CEO-affiliated entities (SpaceX, xAI, X, The Boring Company, and a personal security firm) plus one director-affiliated entity (Redwood Materials/Straubel), on top of a $2.0bn equity investment into a CEO-controlled company (Medium materiality by the net-worth test, ~2.4%) and three now-dismissed-but-appealed derivative suits alleging fiduciary-duty breaches in these same relationships. All disclosed RPTs are reviewed and approved by the Audit Committee under a written RPT Policy requiring pre-approval above $120,000 — a real process, not an absence of governance — but the process has not prevented the network from growing (the $2.0bn SpaceX stake is new since the prior fiscal year) or from becoming a live source of litigation risk. This is flagged per the Red-Flag Trigger Engine as **RF-RPT-002** (promoter-linked RPT) at Medium severity — below the High/Critical bar that would force a harder cap, consistent with `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md`'s finding that the 25%-of-revenue hard disqualifier is not triggered. The core question — is economic value leaking to the CEO's other ventures — is not proven at a magnitude that threatens Tesla's own economics, but the CEO's personal security-firm payments and the unresolved derivative suits are a genuine, if currently small, self-dealing channel that the Audit Committee (chaired by the same director who also chairs the pay-setting Compensation Committee's peer seat) has approved rather than blocked.

## 4. Takeover Defenses & Voting Rights

| Feature | Present? | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Poison pill | Not identified | No shareholder-rights-plan / poison-pill disclosure found in the 10-K/A or 10-Q; **web-checked, 2026-07-24 (unverified beyond absence of reporting)**: no news coverage of a Tesla poison pill found for 2024–2026 | FY2025 10-K/A, Q2 FY26 10-Q (no mention found); Web search, 2026-07-24 |
| Classified / staggered board | **Yes** | Directors serve staggered 3-year terms across three classes: Musk, Denholm, Straubel expire 2026; Hartung, Murdoch, Kimbal Musk expire 2027; Ehrenpreis, Gebbia, Wilson-Thompson expire 2028 — a standing takeover defense that also limits shareholders' ability to replace the full board in one cycle | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 10, director term table |
| Dual-class / unequal voting | No — single class of common stock, one vote per share (the "Voting Agreement" on the 2025 CEO Performance Award's unearned shares makes those shares vote *proportionally* to how all other shares vote, via an irrevocable proxy to Tesla's Secretary — this is a voting-neutrality mechanism, not extra CEO voting power) | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 12, beneficial-ownership footnote (2); Item 11, "2025 CEO Performance Award" |
| Majority vs plurality director voting | Majority-vote standard for uncontested elections, adopted following a shareholder proposal that passed with **98% support at the 2022 annual meeting** — plurality applies only if a contested election occurs (a dissident nominee under advance-notice rules). **Web-sourced, 2026-07-24 (unverified against a current bylaws exhibit not in this pool)**; current status post-Texas-reincorporation not independently confirmed in the data pool | Web search, 2026-07-24, citing Tesla's amended bylaws and 2022 proxy-vote results; not independently verified against the pool's own documents |
| Shareholder ability to call meetings / act by written consent | Not disclosed in this pool (Part III of the 10-K/A does not reproduce the full bylaws). **Follow-up required**: confirm against the current bylaws exhibit | FY2025 10-K/A (silent); not independently confirmed |
| Supermajority charter/bylaw provision | **Yes** — amending the director-term (declassification) provisions requires the affirmative vote of at least 66⅔% of **total outstanding shares** (not just votes cast), a materially higher bar than a simple majority of votes cast. **Web-sourced, 2026-07-24**: this same provision is why the Board states it lacked authority to implement full board declassification even after a 2024 shareholder vote favoring it (see §4A) | Web search, 2026-07-24, citing Tesla's 2024 proxy statement rationale; not independently confirmed against the pool |
| Reincorporation from Delaware to Texas | **Yes**, effective Jun-13-2024 | Confirmed inside the pool: the 10-Q's derivative-litigation note states the suits were filed "[b]efore converting from a Delaware to Texas corporation on June 13, 2024" | Q2 FY26 10-Q, Note (Legal Proceedings), "Certain Derivative Lawsuits in Delaware" |
| Derivative-suit ownership threshold (post-reincorporation) | **Yes** — Tesla's board adopted a bylaw (May-15-2025) requiring a shareholder to hold **3% of outstanding shares** to bring a derivative suit, using a new Texas statute (TBOC §21.552, effective May-14-2025) that caps this threshold at 3%. At the time of adoption, 3% was reported at roughly $32.5bn — a bar only Musk, Vanguard, BlackRock, and State Street individually clear. This directly narrows the tool (a derivative suit like *Tornetta v. Musk*, filed by a shareholder who held nine shares) that had previously forced judicial review of the CEO's own pay package. **Web-sourced, 2026-07-24, not corroborated inside this pool's documents** — a material fact this agent could not verify against a primary filing in the data pool, but is widely reported and directly relevant to the core question this agent answers | Web search, 2026-07-24 (CNBC 2025-05-16, 2025-07-16; FinancialModelingPrep; Bloomberg Law); not independently confirmed against the pool |

**Read on defenses.** The single-class, one-share-one-vote structure and the neutral-voting design of the 2025 CEO award's unearned shares are genuine positives — Tesla does not have Alphabet- or Meta-style super-voting insider control. But the classified board, the 66⅔%-of-outstanding-shares supermajority bar to change it, the 2024 reincorporation from Delaware (where *Tornetta v. Musk* forced judicial scrutiny of the CEO's pay) to Texas, and the subsequent 3%-ownership threshold on derivative suits together form a coherent, sequential entrenchment pattern: each step narrows a specific tool minority shareholders had used, or could use, to challenge board or CEO decisions. Two of these facts (the supermajority provision and the 3% threshold) are not independently confirmed inside this data pool's documents — they are corroborated only by web reporting — and are flagged with a follow-up to verify against the current bylaws exhibit; the reincorporation itself, though, is confirmed directly inside the pool's own 10-Q.

## 4A. Shareholder Voting & Dilution

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| AGM votes against — 2025 CEO Performance Award | Approved with **~75% support**, meaning roughly **25% of voted shares opposed** a $132.3bn maximum grant-date-fair-value award (headline "up to $1 trillion" realizable value cited in press coverage; the pool's own 10-K/A cites the $132.3bn accounting figure) — this clears the module's ">20% votes-against" red-flag threshold. **Web-sourced, 2026-07-24 (CNBC, 2025-11-06)**; the 10-K/A confirms the award was "approved on November 6, 2025 by our shareholders" but does not itself tabulate the vote percentage | FY2025 10-K/A, Item 11 (approval fact only); Web search, 2026-07-24 (CNBC), for the vote percentage |
| Proxy-advisor recommendations | Both ISS and Glass Lewis recommended **against** the 2025 CEO Performance Award ahead of the November 2025 vote, citing dilution and pay-package terms. Shareholders approved it anyway. **Web-sourced, 2026-07-24 (Bloomberg, 2025-10-20; Reuters/Investing.com)** — not corroborated inside this pool | Web search, 2026-07-24; not independently confirmed against the pool |
| Institutional opposition to any resolution | 2024 board-declassification shareholder proposal received 54% support (of votes cast) but was not implemented, because the charter's 66⅔%-of-total-outstanding-shares bar for that specific amendment was not met — a majority of voted shares favored annual elections and the classified board remains in place. **Web-sourced, 2026-07-24**, not corroborated inside this pool | Web search, 2026-07-24; not independently confirmed against the pool |
| Dilution history (preferential allotment, warrants, QIP, ESOP pool) | Shares outstanding rose from 2,532M (Dec-2017) to 3,950M (Jun-2026), a 56% increase, with zero share buybacks recorded in any year — and the 2025 CEO Performance Award layers a further up-to-423.7M shares (~10.7% of shares outstanding at grant) on top of that trend | `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md` §1, citing CIQ Financials_Annual — Balance Sheet and Cash Flow tabs |

High votes-against on the CEO's pay resolution (~25%, above the module's >20% trigger) combined with both major proxy advisors' opposition is flagged as **RF-SHR-001** (High votes against a key resolution) at High severity — the resolution still passed because the CEO and large index-fund holders outweighed the dissenting block, not because the dissent was resolved. The 2024 declassification vote is a related, distinct signal: a majority of voted shares wanted a more accountable board and did not get it, because of a charter provision the board itself has discretion to eventually unwind but has not yet fully done — flagged under the same entrenchment pattern described in §4.

## 5. Minority-Shareholder Protection Read

The board is independent on paper — 77.8% of directors, a separate independent Chair, and four all-independent standing committees, with a real Audit Committee RPT-review process — but its practical record is one of ratifying the CEO's own terms rather than constraining them: it approved a record-setting pay package that both ISS and Glass Lewis recommended against and that roughly a quarter of voted shares opposed, and it has not implemented a board-declassification change that a majority of voted shares supported in 2024, citing a supermajority bylaw threshold. The single biggest self-dealing and entrenchment risk to minority holders is not any one number in the related-party table (all currently below the module's High/Critical thresholds) — it is the sequence of structural changes since 2024: reincorporating from Delaware (where a nine-share investor forced judicial review of Musk's 2018 pay package) to Texas, then adopting a 3%-of-outstanding-shares threshold for derivative suits that only Musk himself and the largest index funds can individually clear, layered on a classified board that a majority of shareholders has already voted, unsuccessfully, to unwind. **Verdict: weak / entrenched.** Two of the most consequential facts in this section (the supermajority bylaw threshold and the 3% derivative-suit bylaw) could not be independently confirmed against a primary document inside this data pool and are corroborated only by dated web reporting — a follow-up to pull the current bylaws exhibit is the single highest-value next step for this specific finding.

## management-governance / 06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md

_Source: `06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md`_

# Candor & Disclosure Quality — TSLA

**Data basis for this report:** verbatim earnings-call transcripts for Q1 2026 (Apr-22-2026) and Q2 2026 (Jul-22-2026) were available in the data pool as legacy Microsoft Word (.doc, mislabeled .rtf) files. The pool's standard text extractor could not render them; this agent independently re-extracted the full verbatim UTF-16LE text stream from each binary file and confirms both are complete, speaker-attributed transcripts (opening remarks plus full analyst Q&A), not paraphrases or press releases. Both are cited below as `[Q1/Q2 2026 Earnings Call transcript, verbatim, <date>]`. No partial-data cap applies to the tone/Q&A-evasiveness components as a result.

## 1. Owning the Misses

| Miss (from 01) | How It Was Communicated | Owned or Obscured? | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| "Return to vehicle-delivery growth in 2025" — missed, deliveries fell 8.5% YoY, second straight down year | Not referenced as a miss in either the Q1 2026 or Q2 2026 call. Q1 2026 opening remarks pivot straight to "a resurgence in demand" language for 2026 with no backward-looking acknowledgment that the 2025 growth promise failed | **Obscured** — reframed forward rather than owned | Q1 2026 Earnings Call transcript, verbatim, Apr-22-2026, CEO/CFO opening remarks (searched; no reference found); Annual_Report_TSLA-Q4-2025.pdf |
| "FSD (Supervised) launch in Europe and China in 2025" — missed outright | The Q4 2025 shareholder update states Tesla "continue[s] to pursue regulatory approval in China and Europe" [per `01_management-and-track-record.md` §4] — language that describes ongoing effort, not a missed 2025 deadline. The Q1 2026 call repeats this framing: "We recently received approvals for FSD in the Netherlands. This sets us up well for EU-wide approval later in Q2" and "the broader [China] approval is still not there, but we're working with the regulators" — again framed as steady progress, with no reference to the original 2025 commitment that was not met | **Obscured** — restated as an in-progress rollout, the original dated 2025 commitment is never named or reconciled against the actual outcome | Q1 2026 Earnings Call transcript, verbatim, Apr-22-2026, CFO prepared remarks; Annual_Report_TSLA-Q4-2025.pdf, FSD/Autonomy section |
| Q2 2026 EPS miss (-38.9% vs. consensus; automotive margin ex-credits fell sequentially 19.2%→16.3%; energy gross margin fell YoY 39.5%/30.3%→20.4%; FCF negative for the first time in eight quarters) | **Split, and the split is the story.** The CFO's prepared remarks own the miss with real specificity and numbers: "we had highlighted in Q1 that we had a $230 million benefit from warranty true-downs and some tariff relief, which did not repeat in Q2… Controlling for the impact of those benefits… margins would have been approximately flat"; a named $240M energy warranty true-up "related to certain vendor cell issues"; explicit FX (~$300M) and Bitcoin (~$100M) losses; explicit statement that FCF "ended up being negative for the quarter." That is a candid, quantified accounting of the drivers. But the CEO's opening remarks — delivered in the same call, on the same quarter — never mention the miss at all: "it's been a great quarter… this is going to be a great year for Tesla, I think one of our best years ever." The two halves of management describe two different quarters | **Split: CFO owns it with numbers; CEO frames the same quarter as unambiguously "great"** | Q2 2026 Earnings Call transcript, verbatim, Jul-22-2026, CEO opening remarks (line 30, 45) and CFO opening remarks (lines 53–58) |

## 2. Non-GAAP / Adjustment Aggressiveness

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Recurring "one-off" addbacks | Two deferred-tax valuation-allowance releases in under three years ($5,927M FY2023; $274M Q2 2026) both boosted GAAP net income and were both excluded from the company's own non-GAAP figure — correctly excluded, but the second occurrence shows this is a repeatable lever, not a true one-time event. Restructuring charges appeared in 5 of the last 9 fiscal years plus 2 of the last 4 quarters, functioning as a recurring cost line despite being labelled "restructuring." Inventory write-downs recurred in every one of the last five quarters shown ($136M/$65M/$49M/$77M/$110M) | earnings/06 §5, §8 |
| GAAP-to-adjusted gap (magnitude / trend) | FY2025 Adjusted EBITDA ($14,596M) exceeds GAAP EBITDA ($10,503M) by $4,093M (+39.0%) — nearly 2.6x this module's 15% flag threshold — driven almost entirely by excluding stock-based compensation, which is itself 65% of FY2025 GAAP operating income. Non-GAAP net income exceeds GAAP net income by 54.4% and non-GAAP EPS exceeds GAAP EPS by 53.7% for the same reason | earnings/06 §4, §7 |
| Does adjusted framing flatter reality? | Yes, materially. SBC is a genuine, recurring cost that dilutes existing shareholders even though it is non-cash, and excluding two-thirds of GAAP operating income from the "adjusted" figure every quarter — every quarter, not just in high-SBC years — means the "Adjusted EBITDA" headline investors see is structurally, not occasionally, more flattering than GAAP. This is disclosed by name in the reconciliation tables (not hidden), but the size and permanence of the gap is aggressive, not conservative | earnings/06 §4, §9 |

## 3. Disclosure Transparency

Segment disclosure is thin by design: Tesla reports only revenue, cost of revenue, and gross profit by segment — never segment operating expense or operating income — so no reader can independently verify the profitability of Robotaxi, Optimus, or Cybercab, the three narratives management leans on hardest [`business-model/03_segment-map.md` §3, carried forward]. That gap has not narrowed as those programs have grown more central to the investment case; if anything it has widened, since Robotaxi and Optimus now carry the bulk of the qualitative "Outlook" commentary (§3 of `01_management-and-track-record.md`) with zero standalone revenue-line disclosure to check it against. Definitional and presentation changes have also been frequent — four distinct changes in roughly 18 months (crypto fair-value accounting, capex redefinition, Adjusted EBITDA redefinition, "other non-current assets" reclassification) — each one restated retroactively and disclosed in a footnote (a genuine positive), but the frequency itself makes period-over-period comparison harder for anyone not tracking every footnote [earnings/06 §6].

## 4. Tone in Bad Times

In the Q2 2026 call — the worst recent quarter on the numbers (EPS missed consensus by -38.9%, automotive and energy gross margins both compressed sequentially/YoY, free cash flow turned negative for the first time in two years) — the tone was split by speaker, not uniformly candid. CEO Elon Musk's opening remarks call it "a great quarter" and forecast "one of our best years ever," without referencing the margin compression, the EPS miss, or the negative free cash flow anywhere in his remarks [Q2 2026 Earnings Call transcript, verbatim, lines 30, 45]. CFO Vaibhav Taneja's remarks, immediately following, are specific and unflinching about the same quarter's problems — naming the non-repeating Q1 benefits, the vendor-driven energy warranty charge, the FX and Bitcoin losses, and the negative FCF by name [lines 53–58]. In the Q&A that followed, none of the six analyst questions asked (Morgan Stanley, BofA, Wells Fargo, LightShed, Truist, Barclays) touched the EPS miss or margin compression at all — every question was about Optimus supply chain, robotaxi regulation, a rumored SpaceX/Tesla merger, capex efficiency, or energy-storage demand — so the miss was never tested under direct questioning; it is not possible to say whether management would have answered the miss directly under pressure because it was never asked. The one deflection on the call was to a direct question about the reported SpaceX/Tesla merger discussions ("we can't talk about combining companies… on an earnings call. It's got to be done with the appropriate process") — a standard legal-boilerplate non-answer on a live, market-relevant question this pool's `12_red-flags-sweep.md` already flags as an unconfirmed but real signal.

## 5. Candor Read

The CFO is a genuinely candid, numbers-first communicator — he names one-off benefits when they inflate a quarter, names them again when they don't repeat, and states negative free cash flow plainly rather than softening it — and that discipline is real evidence of internal financial honesty. But the CEO, who runs the opening remarks and sets the tone investors react to, called the worst quarter in this data pool "great" and "one of our best years ever" without once referencing the miss his own CFO detailed minutes later, and neither call ever reconciles the entirely promotional forward narrative (Optimus, Robotaxi, Terafab) against the two dated 2025 promises this agent's own `01_management-and-track-record.md` independently found were missed. The single biggest candor signal is that split itself: a CFO who owns bad numbers in detail, paired with a CEO whose framing never lets a bad quarter sound bad, on a stock whose valuation depends overwhelmingly on trusting the CEO's forward claims about autonomy and robotics — the exact subject matter under an unresolved securities-fraud class action naming Musk personally (RF-MGT-005, `01_management-and-track-record.md` §4A), which this agent lets weigh against the trust read rather than setting aside. Verdict: **mixed**, with the CEO's promotional framing in the worst quarter and the unresolved integrity signal both pulling toward the low-trust end, offset only by the CFO's genuinely granular, honest quarterly disclosure.
